Compressible Navier-Stokes equation with BV initial data PART II. Global stability
Haitao Wang, Shih-Hsien Yu, Xiongtao Zhang

Abstract
. In previous work [ 21 ] , we studied the local well-posedness of weak solution to the 1-D full compressible Navier-Stokes equation with initial data of small total variation. Specifically, the local existence, the regularity, and the uniqueness in certain function space of the weak solution have been established. The basis for previous study is the precise construction of fundamental solution for heat equation with BV conductivity. In this paper, we continue to investigate the global stability and the time asymptotic behavior of the weak solution. The main step is to construct the “effective Green’s function”, which is the combination of the heat kernel with BV coefficient in short time and the Green’s function around constant state in long time. The former one captures the quasi-linear nature of the system, while the latter one respects the dissipative structure. Then the weak solution is written into an integral system in terms of this “effective Green’s function”, and the time asymptotic behavior is established based on a priori estimate.



1.
Introduction

Consider 1-D full compressible Navier-Stokes equation (NS for short) in Lagrangian coordinate. More precisely, let
v
be the specific volume,
u
be the velocity,
p
be the pressure,
e
be the specific internal energy,
\theta
be the absolute temperature,
\mu
be the viscosity of
u
, and
\kappa
be the heat conductivity of
\theta
, the full NS equation in Lagrangian coordinates reads,

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{t}-u_{x}=0\\
&u_{t}+p_{x}=\left(\frac{\mu u_{x}}{v}\right)_{x}\\
&(e+\frac{1}{2}u^{2})_{t}+(pu)_{x}=\left(\frac{\kappa}{v}\theta_{x}+\frac{\mu}{v}uu_{x}\right)_{x}.\end{aligned}\right.

The above equation (
1.1
) can be derived from the original compressible NS equation in Euler coordinates, we refer the readers to
[
19
]
for details. In order to close the equation (
1.1
), we will consider in this paper the polytropic ideal gas which has the constitutive relations below,

(1.2)

p(v,\theta)=\frac{K\theta}{v},\quad e=c_{v}\theta,

where
K
is a positive constant and
c_{v}
is the heat capacity which is also a positive constant. Note the second equality states that the internal energy depends only on the temperature for ideal gas. To solve Cauchy problem, the system (
1.1
) is supplemented with prescribed initial data
(v_{0},u_{0},\theta_{0})
.

If
(v,u,e)
is a classical solution to (
1.1
), then (
1.1
) is equivalent to the following non-conservative form in terms of function
(v,u,\theta)
,

(1.3)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{t}-u_{x}=0\\
&u_{t}+p_{x}=\left(\frac{\mu u_{x}}{v}\right)_{x}\\
&\theta_{t}+\frac{p}{c_{v}}u_{x}-\frac{\mu}{c_{v}v}\left(u_{x}\right)^{2}=\left(\frac{\kappa}{c_{v}v}\theta_{x}\right)_{x}.\end{aligned}\right.

However, for weak solution, due to the nonlinear term
u^{2}
, the equivalence between (
1.1
) and (
1.3
) is not obvious.

In previous work
[
21
]
, we consider (
1.3
) with initial data being a perturbation around constant state
(1,0,1)
and satisfying the following condition.

(1.4)

\|v^{*}_{0}\|_{BV}+\|u^{*}_{0}\|_{BV}+\|\theta^{*}_{0}\|_{BV}+\|v^{*}_{0}\|_{L^{1}}+\|u^{*}_{0}\|_{L^{1}}+\|\theta^{*}_{0}\|_{L^{1}}<\delta\ll 1.

Here

(v,u,\theta)=(v^{*}+1,u^{*},\theta^{*}+1).

The local solution is constructed through the following iteration scheme,

(1.5)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &v^{n+1}_{t}-u^{n+1}_{x}=0,\\
&u^{n+1}_{t}+p(v^{n},\theta^{n})_{x}=\left(\frac{\mu u^{n+1}_{x}}{v^{n}}\right)_{x},\\
&\theta^{n+1}_{t}+\frac{p(v^{n},\theta^{n})}{c_{v}}u^{n}_{x}-\frac{\mu}{c_{v}v^{n}}(u^{n}_{x})^{2}=\left(\frac{\kappa}{c_{v}v^{n}}\theta^{n+1}_{x}\right)_{x}.\end{aligned}\right.

It is noted the second and third equalities in (
1.5
) are indeed linear inhomogeneous heat equation with BV variable conductivity. The fundamental solution
H(x,t;y,s;\rho)
for heat equation with BV coefficient
\rho(x,t)
, i.e.,

(1.6)

\partial_{t}H(x,t;y,s;\rho)=\partial_{x}\bigl(\rho(x,t)\partial_{x}H(x,t;y,s;\rho)\bigr),\quad H(x,s;y,s;\rho)=\delta(x-y),\quad t>s,

is explicitly constructed in
[
15
]
. Relying on fundamental solution
H(x,t;y,s;\rho)
, the system (
1.5
) is solved by the following integral representation:

(1.7)

\left\{\begin{aligned} v^{n+1}(x,t)&=v_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}u_{x}^{n+1}(x,s)ds,\\
u^{n+1}(x,t)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}H(x,t;y,0;\frac{\mu}{v^{n}})u_{0}(y)dy+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}H_{y}(x,t;y,s;\frac{\mu}{v^{n}})p(v^{n},\theta^{n})(y,s)dyds,\\
\theta^{n+1}(x,t)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}H(x,t;y,0;\frac{\kappa}{c_{v}v^{n}})\theta_{0}(y)dy+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}H(x,t;y,s;\frac{\kappa}{c_{v}v^{n}})\Bigl[-\frac{p(v^{n},\theta^{n})}{c_{v}}u^{n}_{y}+\frac{\mu}{c_{v}}\frac{(u^{n}_{y})^{2}}{v^{n}}\Bigr](y,s)dyds.\end{aligned}\right.

Thanks to the pointwise estimates of heat kernel, the comparison estimates when varying the conductivity coefficient constructed in
[
15
]
, as well as some new estimates concerning the Hölder continuity of heat kernel, in
[
21
]
, we proved the convergence of iteration scheme (
1.7
) in short time, thus obtained the weak solution to system (
1.3
). As the convergence obtained is in strong sense, passing limit
n\to\infty
in (
1.7
), one has the integral representation of the solution
(v,u,\theta)
to (
1.3
). By exploiting the refined estimates of heat kernel, we further get more descriptions of regularity of the weak solution. For instance, we showed that both
u_{t}
and
\theta_{t}
are in
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}
for
t>0
, and the BV norm of
v(\cdot,t)
is Hölder continuous in time. These properties specify the function space which the weak solution belongs to. More importantly, we are able to prove the continuous dependence on initial data of weak solution in that function space, and thus the uniqueness of weak solution. The main results in
[
21
]
is summarized as follows:

Proposition 1.1

(Local Existence,
[
21
]
)
.

Suppose initial data
(v_{0},u_{0},\theta_{0})
satisfy

(1.8)

\left\|v_{0}-1\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}}+\left\|v_{0}\right\|_{BV}+\left\|u_{0}\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}}+\left\|u_{0}\right\|_{BV}+\left\|\theta_{0}-1\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}}+\left\|\theta_{0}\right\|_{BV}\leq\delta,

for
\delta
sufficiently small. Then there exist positive constants
t_{\sharp}
and
C_{\sharp}
such that, the system (
1.3
) admits a weak solution
(v,u,\theta)
for
t\in(0,t_{\sharp})
satisfying the following properties:

(1)

The solution has the following regularity estimates:

(1.9)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\delta>0,\quad 0<t<t_{\sharp}\ll 1,\\
&\max\left\{\begin{aligned} &\left\|u(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}},\ \left\|u(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}},\ \left\|u_{x}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}},\ \sqrt{t}\left\|u_{x}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}},\\
&\sqrt{t}\left\|u_{t}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}},\ t\left\|u_{t}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\end{aligned}\right\}\leq 2C_{\sharp}\delta,\\
&\max\left\{\begin{aligned} &\left\|\theta(\cdot,t)-1\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}},\ \left\|\theta(\cdot,t)-1\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}},\ \left\|\theta_{x}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}},\ \sqrt{t}\left\|\theta_{x}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}},\\
&\sqrt{t}\left\|\theta_{t}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}},\ t\left\|\theta_{t}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\end{aligned}\right\}\leq 2C_{\sharp}\delta,\\
&\max\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathscr{D}}|v_{x}(x,t)|dx,\ \left\|v(\cdot,t)-1\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}},\ \left\|v(\cdot,t)-1\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}},\ \sqrt{t}\left\|v_{t}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\right\}\leq 2C_{\sharp}\delta,\\
&v^{*}=v^{*}_{c}+v^{*}_{d},\quad v^{*}_{d}(x,t)=\sum_{z<x,z\in\mathscr{D}}v^{*}\Big|_{z^{-}}^{z^{+}}h(x-z),\quad\text{$v_{c}^{*}$ is AC},\\
&\left|v(\cdot,t)\Big|_{x=z^{-}}^{x=z^{+}}\right|\leq 2\left|v^{*}_{0}(\cdot)\Big|_{x=z^{-}}^{x=z^{+}}\right|,\quad z\in\mathscr{D},\end{aligned}\right.

where
h(x)
is the Heaviside step function, and
AC
means absolutely continuous.

(2)

The fluxes of
u
and
\theta
are both global Lipschitz continuous with respect to
x
for any
t>0
, where the flux of
u
and
\theta
in (
1.3
) are defined as follows,

\bigl[\mbox{flux of }u\bigr]=\frac{\mu u_{x}}{v}-p,\qquad\bigl[\mbox{flux of }\theta\bigr]=\frac{\kappa}{c_{v}v}\theta_{x}-\int_{-\infty}^{x}\left(\frac{p}{c_{v}}u_{z}-\frac{\mu}{c_{v}v}\left(u_{z}\right)^{2}\right)dz.

(3)

The specific volume
v(x,t)
has the following Hölder continuity in time properties for
0\leq s<t
,

(1.10)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathscr{D}}|v_{x}(x,t)-v_{x}(x,s)|dx\leq 2C_{\sharp}\delta\frac{(t-s)|\log(t-s)|}{\sqrt{t}},\\
&\sum_{z\in\mathscr{D}}\left|v_{x}(\cdot,t)\Big|_{z^{-}}^{z^{+}}-v_{x}(\cdot,s)\Big|_{z^{-}}^{z^{+}}\right|\leq 2C_{\sharp}\delta\frac{t-s}{\sqrt{t}},\\
&\|v(\cdot,t)-v(\cdot,s)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 2C_{\sharp}\delta\frac{t-s}{\sqrt{t}},\\
&\|v(\cdot,t)-v(\cdot,s)\|_{L^{1}}\leq 2C_{\sharp}\delta(t-s).\end{aligned}\right.

Here
\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathscr{D}}
is defined in
(
2.3
), stands for integration of absolutely
continuous part of a BV function.

Remark 1.1
.

The regularity of
u_{t}
and
\theta_{t}
indeed provide the regularity of the fluxes in (
1.3
). In other words, although
u_{x}
,
\theta_{x}
and
v
might be discontinuous with respect to
x
individually, the fluxes as combinations of these discontinuous quantities are Lipschitz continuous and differentiable a.e. Therefore, all the derivatives in the equation (
1.3
) are in strong sense a.e.,
which also implies
(v,u,e+u^{2}/2)
is a weak solution to the original system (
1.1
), where
e
is defined through (
1.2
). This shows for the solution constructed in Proposition
1.1
, systems (
1.1
) and (
1.3
) are equivalent.

It immediately follows that the solution
(v,u,\theta)
constructed in Proposition
1.1
belongs to the following function space:

(1.11)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &v(x,t)-1\in C\left([0,t_{\sharp});L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\cap BV\right),\\
&u(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\right),\quad\sqrt{t}u_{x}(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\right),\\
&\sqrt{t}u_{t}(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\right),\quad tu_{t}(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\right),\\
&\theta(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\right),\quad\sqrt{t}\theta_{x}(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\right),\\
&\sqrt{t}\theta_{t}(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\right),\quad t\theta_{t}(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\right).\end{aligned}\right.

It is worth mentioning that, in the above space,
v
is continuous in
L^{1}
,
L^{\infty}
and
BV
sense around
t=0
, which exactly holds for the solution constructed in Proposition
1.1
due to (
1.10
).

Moreover, based on the above regularity estimates, the uniqueness of the constructed weak solution in Proposition
1.1
is also obtained. Actually, owning to the continuity of
v
in
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}\cap BV
in short time, one can construct heat kernel
H(x,t;y,s;\frac{\mu}{v})
and
H(x,t;y,s;\frac{\kappa}{c_{v}v})
, which yields an integral representation of
(v,u,\theta)
, then the uniqueness essentially follows from comparison estimates. Precisely, we have

Proposition 1.2

(Stability and uniqueness,
[
21
]
)
.

Suppose there are two solutions
(v^{a},u^{a},\theta^{a})
and
(v^{b},u^{b},\theta^{b})
to the Navier-Stokes equation (
1.3
) with the regularity properties stated in (
1.11
), and their initial data both satisfy for small
\delta_{*}
,

\|v_{0}\|_{BV}+\|u_{0}\|_{BV}+\|\theta_{0}\|_{BV}+\|v_{0}-1\|_{L^{1}}+\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}}+\|\theta_{0}-1\|_{L^{1}}<\delta_{*}\ll 1,

Then, there exists positive constants
t_{*}
and
C_{\flat}
such that for
0<t<t_{*}
,

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|v^{a}-v^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|v^{a}-v^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|v^{a}-v^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{BV}

\displaystyle+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|u^{a}-u^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|u^{a}-u^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{|\log\tau|}\left(u_{x}^{a}-u_{x}^{b}\right)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{u^{a}_{x}-u^{b}_{x}}{|\log\tau|}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}

\displaystyle+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{\theta^{a}-\theta^{b}}{|\log\tau|}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\theta^{a}-\theta^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{|\log\tau|}\left(\theta^{a}_{x}-\theta^{b}_{x}\right)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{\theta^{a}_{x}-\theta^{b}_{x}}{|\log\tau|}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\flat}\Big(\|\theta^{a}_{0}-\theta^{b}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\theta^{a}_{0}-\theta^{b}_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{x}}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+\|u^{a}_{0}-u^{b}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|u^{a}_{0}-u^{b}_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{x}}+\|v^{a}_{0}-v^{b}_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{x}}+\|v^{a}_{0}-v^{b}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|v^{a}_{0}-v^{b}_{0}\|_{BV}\Big),

where

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|v^{a}-v^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}\equiv\sup_{t\in\left(0,t_{*}\right)}\lVert v^{a}(\cdot,t)-v^{b}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

and similarly for other
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}
norms. This immediately implies the uniqueness of the weak solution.

In this paper, we are curious about whether the local-in-time solution constructed in Propositions
1.1
and
1.2
can be extended to a global one and what the time asymptotic behavior it has. The heat kernel with BV conductivity is effective for local well-posedness since it accurately captures the quasi-linear nature of the system (
1.1
). On the other hand, concerning large time behavior, the hyperbolic-parabolic structure plays a key role, which is, however, not reflected in the heat kernel. This motivates us to introduce a new ingredient, Green’s function for linearized system around equilibrium state.

In order to preserve the conservative form of equation (
1.1
), we define the following state variables,

(1.12)

E=e+\frac{1}{2}u^{2},\quad U=(v,u,E),\quad p(v,e(E,u))\simeq\frac{E-\frac{1}{2}u^{2}}{v},\quad e_{u}=-u,\quad e_{E}=1.

Then, the equation (
1.1
) can be rewritten as following conservation form equation with variables in (
1.12
),

(1.13)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{t}-u_{x}=0\\
&u_{t}+p_{v}v_{x}+p_{e}e_{u}u_{x}+p_{e}e_{E}E_{x}=\left(\frac{\mu u_{x}}{v}\right)_{x}\\
&E_{t}+up_{v}v_{x}+(p+up_{e}e_{u})u_{x}+up_{e}e_{E}E_{x}=\left(\frac{\kappa\theta_{e}e_{u}+\mu u}{v}u_{x}+\frac{\kappa\theta_{e}e_{E}}{v}E_{x}\right)_{x}.\end{aligned}\right.

We can also write the system into a more compact form as follows,

U_{t}+F(U)_{x}=(B(U)U_{x})_{x}\Longleftrightarrow U_{t}+F^{\prime}(U)U_{x}=(B(U)U_{x})_{x}.

where
U
,
F
,
F^{\prime}(U)
and
B
are defined as below,

U=\begin{pmatrix}v\\
u\\
E\end{pmatrix},\quad F(U)=\begin{pmatrix}-u\\
p\\
pu\end{pmatrix},\quad F^{\prime}(U)=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0\\
p_{v}&-p_{e}u&p_{e}\\
p_{v}u&p-p_{e}u^{2}&p_{e}u\end{pmatrix},\quad B(U)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&\frac{\mu}{v}&0\\
0&\left(\frac{\mu}{v}-\frac{\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}\right)u&\frac{\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}\end{pmatrix}.

Now we consider the linearization of equation (
1.13
) around a constant state
\bar{U}
. More precisely, let
U=\bar{U}+V
, then we have

(1.14)

V_{t}+F^{\prime}(\bar{U})V_{x}-B(\bar{U})V_{xx}=[N_{1}(V;\bar{U})+N_{2}(V;\bar{U})]_{x},

where
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are nonlinear terms from the hyperbolic and parabolic parts respectively,

(1.15)

N_{1}(V;\bar{U})=-[F(\bar{U}+V)-F(\bar{U})-F^{\prime}(\bar{U})V],\quad N_{2}(V;\bar{U})=B(U)-B(\bar{U}).

The linearization of the nonlinear system (
1.13
) is obtained if we get rid of the right hand side nonlinear terms in (
1.14
). For the linearized equation, we follow the methodology in
[
14
,
15
]
to obtain the pointwise estimates of Green’s function. More precisely, we use the spectral analysis to extract the singularities from the high frequency modes (see Theorem
3.1
and Theorem
3.2
for details). Then, for the regular part, we apply the long-short wave decomposition and weighted energy estimate to construct the desired results (see Theorem
3.3
for details).

With heat kernel with BV coefficient and Green’s function in hand, we combine them together to take care both local and global structure of system (
1.1
) simultaneously. We interpolate the heat kernel and Green’s function together to construct the “effective Green’s function”, which yields the representation of the solutions to (
1.13
). As we already proved the regularity of the weak solution to (
1.1
), thus the equation (
1.13
) is equivalent to (
1.1
) and (
1.3
), which means we actually obtain another representation of the solutions to (
1.1
) and (
1.3
). The effective Green’s function can be viewed as “global nonlinear Green’s function” in the sense that it respects both local and large time structure of the system. The representations via effective Green’s function have two benefits: in short time, the heat kernels for variable conductivity coefficients lead to small errors and avoid the non-integrable singularities; while for large time, the dissipation structure of the Green’s function guarantees the global estimates. Eventually, we apply the continuity argument to obtain the global well-posedness theory (see Theorem
4.1
for details). The large time behavior of the solution is given as below:

Theorem 1.1

(Main Theorem: Large Time Behavior)
.

Suppose initial data
(v_{0},u_{0},\theta_{0})
of Navier-Stokes equation (
1.3
) satisfy

\left\|v_{0}-1\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}}+\left\|v_{0}\right\|_{BV}+\left\|u_{0}\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}}+\left\|u_{0}\right\|_{BV}+\left\|\theta_{0}-1\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}}+\left\|\theta_{0}\right\|_{BV}\leq\delta,

for
\delta
sufficiently small. Then the solution constructed in Propositions
1.1
and
1.2
exists globally in time, and there exists positive constant
C_{\sharp}
such that,

\displaystyle\left\|\sqrt{t+1}(v(\cdot,t)-1)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\left\|\sqrt{t+1}u(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\left\|\sqrt{t+1}(\theta(\cdot,t)-1)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt+\left\|\sqrt{t}u_{x}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\left\|\sqrt{t}\theta_{x}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C_{\sharp}\delta~\mbox{ for }t\in(0,+\infty).

Remark 1.2
.

It is even possible to establish the space-time pointwise estimate of the global solution for BV data, provided the initial data satisfy certain space decay assumption. This will be pursued in the future work.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce some preliminary concepts and various estimates of the heat kernel with variable coefficient. The essential ideas arise from
[
15
]
, and thus we only list the estimates for variable coefficient heat kernel that will be used in later sections. For the detailed proof, the reader is referred to
[
15
,
21
]
. In Section
3
, we construct the Green’s function for the linearzied system (
1.14
) around constant state. In Section
4
, we first interpolate heat kernel and Green’s function to have the “effective Green’s function”, which yields an integral representation of the solution. The time asymptotic behavior then follow from precise estimate of effective Green’s function and a priori estimate.

2.
Preliminary

In this section, we will provide some preliminary concepts and results that will be used in the later sections. We first give definitions of the weak and a regularity property, then we introduce the pointwise and comparison estimates of the heat kernel for BV coefficients.

2.1.
Weak solutions

Definition 2.1
.

A tuple
(v,u,\theta)
is a weak solution to the equation (
1.3
) in the distribution sense if for any test function
\varphi(x,t)\in C^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,+\infty))
,

(2.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\varphi_{x}u-\varphi_{t}v\right]dxdt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi(x,0)v(x,0)dx,\\
&\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\varphi_{x}\left(\frac{\mu u_{x}}{v}-p\right)-\varphi_{t}u\right]dxdt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi(x,0)u(x,0)dx,\\
&\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\varphi_{x}\left(\frac{\kappa}{c_{v}v}\theta_{x}\right)+\varphi\left(\frac{p}{c_{v}}u_{x}-\frac{\mu}{c_{v}v}\left(u_{x}\right)^{2}\right)-\varphi_{t}\theta\right]dxdt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi(x,0)\theta(x,0)dx.\end{aligned}\right.

The weak solutions to the linearized equation (
1.5
) and (
1.6
) can be
defined similarly.

Proposition 2.1
.

For the weak solution
(v,u,\theta)
of (
1.3
),
the function
(v,u,c_{v}\theta+u^{2}/2)
is a weak solution of (
1.1
) if
it is satisfies:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &v(x,t)-1\in C\left([0,t_{\sharp});L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\cap BV\right),\\
&u(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\right),\quad\sqrt{t}u_{x}(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\right),\\
&\sqrt{t}u_{t}(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\right),\quad tu_{t}(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\right),\\
&\theta(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\right),\quad\sqrt{t}\theta_{x}(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\right),\\
&\sqrt{t}\theta_{t}(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\right),\quad t\theta_{t}(x,t)\in L^{\infty}\left(0,t_{\sharp};L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\right).\end{aligned}\right.

2.2.
Heat kernel estiamtes

In this subsection, one provides estimates for the fundamental
solution
H(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho)
(heat kernel) of
a heat equation with a BV function coefficient
\rho
in
x
,

\partial_{t}+\partial_{x}\rho(x,t)\partial_{x})u=0.

A BV function
f
can be decomposed as the sum of an absolutely continuous part and a discrete part,

\displaystyle f(x)=f_{c}(x)+f_{d}(x),\quad\text{$f_{c}$ is absolutely continuous},\quad f_{d}(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathscr{D},\alpha<x}f(y)\Bigr|_{y=\alpha-}^{y=\alpha+}h(x-\alpha),

where
\mathscr{D}
is the discontinuity set of
f(x)
and
h(\cdot)
is the Heaviside function. The total variation can also be represented as

(2.2)

\lVert f\rvert_{BV}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lvert\partial_{x}u_{c}\rvert dx+\left.\left.\sum_{\alpha\in\mathscr{D}}\right|f_{d}(x)\big|_{x=\alpha^{-}}^{x=\alpha^{+}}\right|.

Hereafter, we also adopt the notation when integrating the derivative of a BV function

(2.3)

\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathscr{D}}\lvert\partial_{x}f\rvert dx\equiv\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lvert\partial_{x}f_{c}\rvert dx.

The fundamental solution
H(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho)
is defined as a weak
solution of the initial value problem:

(2.4)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\left(\partial_{t}-\partial_{x}\rho(x,t)\partial_{x}\right)H(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho)=0,\quad t>t_{0},\\
&H(x,t_{0};y,t_{0};\rho)=\delta(x-y),\end{aligned}\right.

i.e.

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(-\phi_{t}(x,t)H(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho)+\phi_{x}(x,t)\rho(x,t)H_{x}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho)\right)dxdt=-\phi(y,t_{0})

for all test functions
\phi
;
and
\rho(x,t)
is assumed to satisfy for some positive constants
\bar{\rho}
and
\delta^{*}
,

(2.5)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\|\rho(\cdot)-\bar{\rho}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\delta^{*},\quad\|\rho(\cdot,t)\|_{BV}\leq\delta^{*},\quad\|\rho_{t}(\cdot,t)\|_{\infty}\leq\delta^{*}\max\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}},1\right),\quad 0<\delta^{*}\ll 1,\\
&\mathscr{D}\equiv\left\{z\ |\ \text{$\rho(z,t)$ is not continuous at
$z$}\right\}\text{ is invariant in }t.\end{aligned}\right.

The construction of heat kernel for variable coefficient was introduced in
[
15
]
. Roughly speaking, the construction consists of three steps. The first step is to construct the heat kernel for coefficient being step function in space and independent of time. This is achieved by introducing the Laplace wave train and write the solution in transformed variable by stochastic formulation. Then various detailed derivatives estimate can be obtained through this representation. In the second step, one uses a sequence of step functions to approximate general BV function, and prove the convergence of heat kernel sequence. Lastly, one uses frozen coefficient technique to represent the time-dependent heat kernel by integral equation in terms of time-independent heat kernel. Although the general framework is given in
[
15
]
, in the current work, we need more detailed estimates than before.

The results are summarized in the following several Lemmas.

Lemma 2.1

(Liu-Yu,
[
15
]
)
.

Suppose the conditions of
\rho
in (
2.5
) hold. Then, there
exist positive constants
C_{*}
and
t_{\sharp}\ll 1
such that the weak
solution of (
2.4
) exists and satisfies
for
t\in(t_{0},t_{0}+t_{\sharp})

(2.6)

\displaystyle\left|H(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho)\right|\leq C_{*}\frac{e^{-\frac{\left(x-y\right)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}}{\sqrt{t-t_{0}}},

(2.7)

\displaystyle\left|H_{x}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho)\right|+\left|H_{y}(x,t;y,\mu)\right|\leq C_{*}\frac{e^{-\frac{\left(x-y\right)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}}{t-t_{0}},

(2.8)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}H_{x}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho)d\tau\right|,\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}H_{x}(x,t;y,s;\rho)ds\right|\leq C_{*}e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}.

Lemma 2.2

(
[
20
]
)
.

Under the same consideration as in
Lemma
2.1
, for
t\in(t_{0},t_{0}+t_{\sharp})

the weak
solution of (
2.4
) satisfies

(2.9)

\displaystyle\left|H_{xy}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho)\right|+\left|H_{t}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho)\right|\leq C_{*}\frac{e^{-\frac{\left(x-y\right)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}}{(t-t_{0})^{\frac{3}{2}}},

(2.10)

\displaystyle\left|H_{ty}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho)\right|\leq C_{*}\frac{e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}}{(t-t_{0})^{2}},

(2.11)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}H_{xy}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho)d\tau-\frac{\delta(x-y)}{\rho(x,t_{0})}-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\frac{\rho(x,t_{0})-\rho(x,\tau)}{\rho(x,t_{0})}H_{xy}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho)d\tau\right|\leq C_{*}\frac{e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}}{\sqrt{t-t_{0}}},

(2.12)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}H_{xy}(x,t;y,s;\rho)ds+\frac{\delta(x-y)}{\rho(y,t)}-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\frac{\rho(y,t)-\rho(y,s)}{\rho(y,t)}H_{xy}(x,t;y,s;\rho)ds\right|\leq C_{*}\frac{e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}}{\sqrt{t-t_{0}}},

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t}H_{xx}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho)d\tau=-\frac{\delta(x-y)}{\rho(x,t_{0})}-\frac{1}{\rho(x,t_{0})}\partial_{x}\left[\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left(\rho(x,\tau)-\rho(x,t_{0})\right)H_{x}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho)d\tau\right]

(2.13)

\displaystyle\hskip 110.00017pt+O(1)\left(|\partial_{x}\rho(x,t_{0})|e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}+\frac{e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}}{\sqrt{t-t_{0}}}\right),\qquad\mbox{ for }x\notin\mathscr{D},\,

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t}H_{xxy}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho)d\tau=\frac{1}{\rho(x,t_{0})}\left[\delta^{\prime}(x-y)-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\partial_{x}\Big[\big(\rho(x,\tau)-\rho(x,t_{0})\big)H_{xy}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho)\Big]d\tau\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 110.00017pt-\frac{\partial_{x}\rho(x,t_{0})}{\rho^{2}(x,t_{0})}\left[\delta(x-y)-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big(\rho(x,\tau)-\rho(x,t_{0})\big)H_{xy}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho)d\tau\right]

(2.14)

\displaystyle\hskip 110.00017pt+O(1)\left(|\partial_{x}\rho(x,t_{0})|\frac{e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}}{\sqrt{t-t_{0}}}+\frac{e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}}{t-t_{0}}\right),\qquad\mbox{ for }x\notin\mathscr{D},

(2.15)

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t}H_{t}(x,t;y,s;\rho)ds=H(x,t-t_{0};y;\mu^{t})-\delta(x-y)+O(1)\delta_{*}e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}},\qquad\mbox{where }\mu^{t}(\cdot)\equiv\rho(\cdot,t).

Notice that the estimates for the terms involving twice
x-
derivatives do not hold when
x\in\mathscr{D}
, which is due to the presence of Dirac-delta functions in
H_{xx}
if
x\in\mathscr{D}
. Moreover, the zeroth order estimate actually can be extended to be global in time, while the higher order estimates are obtained only for local time so far.

Lemma 2.3

(
[
20
]
, Hölder continuity in time)
.

Suppose the conditions in (
2.5
) hold for
\rho
. Then the following estimates hold when
t_{0}<s<t\ll 1
,

\displaystyle\left\|H_{x}(\cdot,t;y,t_{0};\rho)-H_{x}(\cdot,s;y,t_{0};\rho)\right\|_{\infty}\leq C_{*}\frac{(t-s)\left|\log(t-s)\right|}{(s-t_{0})(t-t_{0})},

\displaystyle\left\|H_{x}(\cdot,t;y,t_{0};\rho)-H_{x}(\cdot,s;y,t_{0};\rho)\right\|_{1}\leq C_{*}\frac{(t-s)\left|\log(t-s)\right|}{\sqrt{s-t_{0}}(t-t_{0})},

\displaystyle\left\|H_{xy}(\cdot,t;y,t_{0};\rho)-H_{xy}(\cdot,s;y,t_{0};\rho)\right\|_{\infty}\leq C_{*}\frac{(t-s)\left|\log(t-s)\right|}{(s-t_{0})^{3/2}(t-t_{0})},

\displaystyle\left\|H_{xy}(\cdot,t;y,t_{0};\rho)-H_{xy}(\cdot,s;y,t_{0};\rho)\right\|_{1}\leq C_{*}\frac{(t-s)\left|\log(t-s)\right|}{(s-t_{0})(t-t_{0})}.

We need the comparison estimate between two heat kernels with different conductivity coefficients. In the following we denote that

(2.16)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}\equiv\sup_{\sigma\in(0,t_{\sharp})}\|f(\cdot,\sigma)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}},\quad{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}\equiv\sup_{\sigma\in(0,t_{\sharp})}\|f(\cdot,\sigma)\|_{L^{1}_{x}},\quad{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{BV}\equiv\sup_{\sigma\in(0,t_{\sharp})}\|f(\cdot,\sigma)\|_{BV}.

Lemma 2.4

(Comparison estimates, Corollaries 4.4 and 4.5 in
[
15
]
)
.

Suppose that the conditions in (
2.5
) hold for
\rho^{a}
and

\rho^{b}
. Then, there exist positive constants
t_{\sharp}\ll 1
and

C_{*}
such that for
t\in(t_{0},t_{0}+t_{\sharp})

\displaystyle\left|H(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho^{b})-H(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho^{a})\right|\leq C_{*}\frac{e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}}{\sqrt{t-t_{0}}}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty},

\displaystyle\left|H_{x}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho^{a})-H_{x}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho^{b})\right|,\;\left|H_{y}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho^{a})-H_{y}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho^{b})\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{*}\frac{e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}}{t-t_{0}}\left[\left|\log(t-t_{0})\right|{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{BV}+\sqrt{t-t_{0}}\Big({\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}+\left|\log t\right|{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{\left|\log\tau\right|}\partial_{\tau}\left[\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right]\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}\Big)\right],

\displaystyle\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left[H_{x}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho^{a})-H_{x}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho^{b})\right]d\tau\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{*}e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{BV}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{\lvert\log\tau\rvert}\partial_{\tau}(\rho^{a}-\rho^{b})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}\right].

Lemma 2.5

(
[
20
]
)
.

Under the considerations in Lemma

2.4
, there exist positive constants
t_{\sharp}\ll 1
and

C_{*}
such that for
t\in(t_{0},t_{0}+t_{\sharp})

\displaystyle\left|H_{xy}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho^{a})-H_{xy}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho^{b})\right|,\;\left|H_{t}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho^{a})-H_{t}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho^{b})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{*}\frac{e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}}{(t-t_{0})^{3/2}}\left[\left|\log(t-t_{0})\right|{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{BV}+\sqrt{t-t_{0}}\Big({\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}+\left|\log t\right|{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{\left|\log\tau\right|}\partial_{\tau}\left[\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right]\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}\Big)\right],

\displaystyle\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left[H_{y}(x,t;y,s;\rho^{a})-H_{y}(x,t;y,s;\rho^{b})\right]ds\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq C_{*}e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{BV}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{|\log\tau|}\partial_{\tau}(\rho^{a}-\rho^{b})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}\right],

\displaystyle\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left[H_{x}(x,t;y,s;\rho^{a})-H_{x}(x,t;y,s;\rho^{b})\right]ds\right|,\;\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left[H_{y}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho^{a})-H_{y}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho^{b})\right]d\tau\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq C_{*}e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{BV}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{|\log\tau|}\partial_{\tau}(\rho^{a}-\rho^{b})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}\right],

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left[H_{xy}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho^{a})-H_{xy}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho^{b})\right]d\tau

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{\rho^{a}(x,t_{0})}-\frac{1}{\rho^{b}(x,t_{0})}\right]\delta(x-y)

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left[\frac{\rho^{a}(x,\tau)-\rho^{a}(x,t_{0})}{\rho^{a}(x,t_{0})}H_{xy}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho^{a})-\frac{\rho^{b}(x,\tau)-\rho^{b}(x,t_{0})}{\rho^{b}(x,t_{0})}H_{xy}(x,\tau;y,t_{0};\rho^{b})\right]d\tau

\displaystyle\quad+O(1)\frac{e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}}{\sqrt{t-t_{0}}}\left[\left|\log(t-t_{0})\right|{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{BV}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{|\log\tau|}\partial_{\tau}(\rho^{a}-\rho^{b})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}\right],

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left[H_{xy}(x,t;y,s;\rho^{a})-H_{xy}(x,t;y,s;\rho^{b})\right]ds

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{\rho^{a}(y,t)}-\frac{1}{\rho^{b}(y,t)}\right]\delta(x-y)

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left[\frac{\rho^{a}(y,t)-\rho^{a}(y,s)}{\rho^{a}(y,t)}H_{xy}(x,t;y,s;\rho^{a})-\frac{\rho^{b}(y,t)-\rho^{b}(y,s)}{\rho^{b}(y,t)}H_{xy}(x,t;y,s;\rho^{b})\right]ds

\displaystyle+O(1)\frac{e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{C_{*}(t-t_{0})}}}{\sqrt{t-t_{0}}}\left[\left|\log(t-t_{0})\right|{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{BV}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\rho^{a}-\rho^{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{\lvert\log\tau\rvert}\partial_{\tau}(\rho^{a}-\rho^{b})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}\right].

Lastly, according to the symmetry of the heat equation, one has some symmetric properties of the heat kernel. As the proof can be constructed directly from proper integrals of the equation (
2.4
), we omit the details and provide the following results.

Lemma 2.6
.

For the heat equation in conservative form (
2.4
), the solution has the following properties,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\int_{\mathbb{R}}H(x,t;y,\tau;\rho)dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}H(x,t;y,\tau;\rho)dy=1,\\
&\int_{\mathbb{R}}H_{x}(x,t;y,\tau;\rho)dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}H_{x}(x,t;y,\tau;\rho)dy=0,\\
&\int_{\mathbb{R}}H_{y}(x,t;y,\tau;\rho)dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}H_{y}(x,t;y,\tau;\rho)dy=0,\\
&\int_{\mathbb{R}}H_{t}(x,t;y,\tau;\rho)dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}H_{t}(x,t;y,\tau;\rho)dy=0,\\
&\int_{\mathbb{R}}H_{\tau}(x,t;y,\tau;\rho)dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}H_{\tau}(x,t;y,\tau;\rho)dy=0.\end{aligned}\right.

Remark 2.1
.

According to
[
15
]
, two remarks should be mentioned:

(1)

In order to balance the equation (
2.4
),
\rho(x,t)H_{x}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho)
is actually continuous with respect to
x
. When consider the backward equation, one also has
\rho(y,t_{0})H_{y}(x,t;y,t_{0};\rho)
is continuous with respect to
y
.

(2)

The weak solution of the heat equation (
2.4
) can be defined similarly as Definition
2.1
. In fact, if the equation (
2.4
) has a source term in the following conservative form,

u_{t}(x,t)=\left(\rho(x,t)u_{x}(x,t)+g(x,t)\right)_{x},

then the mild solution constructed by heat kernel and Duhamel’s principle is also a weak solution to the above equation in the distribution sense, provided
g(x,t)
is a BV function with respect to
x
. Furthermore, the flux term
\left(\rho(x,t)u_{x}(x,t)+g(x,t)\right)
is continuous with respect to
x
if one of the following two properties holds,

(a)

g(x,t)
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to
x
, i.e.,

\|g_{x}(\cdot,t)\|_{\infty}<+\infty.

(b)

g(x,t)
is Hölder continuous with respect to
t
in the sense that

\lvert g(x,t)-g(x,s)\rvert\leq C\frac{(t-s)^{\alpha}}{s^{\alpha}},\quad 0<s<t,\quad 0<\alpha<1.

3.
Green’s function

As the local-in-time weak solution is established, the next two sections are devoted to the global-in-time behavior of the solution. In this section, we will prepare the tools to capture the large time structure of the system (
1.1
), that is, the Green’s function of the system (
1.14
). The Green’s function
\mathbb{G}(x,t;\bar{U})
of the linearized equation of (
1.14
) is the solution of the following system,

(3.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{G}(x,t;\bar{U})=\left(-F^{\prime}(\bar{U})\partial_{x}+B(\bar{U})\partial_{xx}\right)\mathbb{G}(x,t;\bar{U}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}(x,0;\bar{U})=\delta(x)I,

where
I
is the
3\times 3
identity matrix and
\delta(x)
is the Dirac-delta function. Then, with Fourier transformation, we can obtain the following equation in transformed variable,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\eta,t;\bar{U})=\left(-i\eta F^{\prime}(\bar{U})-\eta^{2}B(\bar{U})\right)\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\eta,t;\bar{U}),

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\eta,0;\bar{U})=I.

We shall first show the eigenvalues of the matrix
\left(-i\eta F^{\prime}(\bar{U})-\eta^{2}B(\bar{U})\right)
are distinct and thus represent the Green’s function by the state-transition matrix.

To obtain the Green’s function in terms of physical variables, we basically follow the approach introduced in
[
4
,
15
]
to split the Green’s function into singular part and regular part. Roughly speaking, the singular part corresponds to large
|\eta|
, which represents the singularity in the Green’s function; while the regular part corresponds to small
|\eta|
, which dominates the large time behavior of Green’s function. We shall construct the point-wise estimates for each of them.

In the following, all the norms of the matrix can be viewed as the trace norm and we define the
L^{2}
norm of a matrix valued function
A(x)
as follows,

(3.3)

\|A(x)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}}tr\left(A^{\mathsf{T}}A\right)dx}.

3.1.
Analysis on eigenvalues

In this part, we will give a preliminary estimate on the eigenvalues of the matrix
\left(-i\eta F^{\prime}(\bar{U})-\eta^{2}B(\bar{U})\right)
. In
[
15
]
, the authors studied p-system and construct the exact formula of the eigenvalues. However, for full Navier-Stokes equations, the exact expression is too complicated to do further analysis. Therefore, we will mainly consider the expansion of the eigenvalues at infinity and zero respectively.

3.1.1.
Green’s function in frequency space

We first show the three eigenvalues mentioned before are distinct, and thus provide the spectral representation of the Green’s function by the resolution. We first provide the characteristic equation from the determinant of the matrix
I\lambda-\left(-i\eta F^{\prime}(\bar{U})-\eta^{2}B(\bar{U})\right)
. Actually, we have

(3.4)

\displaystyle\det(\lambda I+i\eta F^{\prime}(\bar{U})+\eta^{2}B(\bar{U}))=\eta^{2}\lambda pp_{e}+\frac{\left(\lambda\left(\eta^{2}\mu+\lambda v\right)-\eta^{2}vp_{v}\right)\left(\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}+\lambda v\right)}{v^{2}}=0,

where all the state variables take values at
\bar{U}
. According to the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra, there are three roots of (
3.4
) and we denote them by
\lambda_{j}
with
j=1,2,3
. Moreover, from the Residue Theorem and Rouché’s Theorem, the eigenvalues continuously depend on the coefficients. As the coefficients are polynomials of
\eta
, we immediately conclude the eigenvalues continuously depend on
\eta
. In the following Lemma, we will further see these three eigenvalues are distinct when
\eta
is near real axis.

Lemma 3.1
.

There exists a sufficiently small positive constant
\sigma_{0}
such that, the matrix
\left(-i\eta F^{\prime}(\bar{U})-\eta^{2}B(\bar{U})\right)
has three distinct eigenvalues for
|\eta|>0
and
|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}
.

Proof.

It is enough to show the equation (
3.4
) admits three distinct solutions for
\eta
near the real line. As (
3.4
) is of order three, it has one real root and two conjugate complex roots, or it has three real roots. If there are one real root and two conjugate roots, the three roots must be distinct. Thus we only need to consider the case when all the three roots are real.

Let’s check the necessary condition for existence of multiple roots. Suppose the three roots are
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
and
\lambda_{3}
, and
\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{3}
. Then the characteristic equation can be expressed as below,

(3.5)

(\lambda-\lambda_{1})(\lambda-\lambda_{2})^{2}=0,\quad\lambda_{2}\neq 0,

where the multiple root
\lambda_{2}\neq 0
since the coefficients in (
3.4
) are nonzero when
\eta\neq 0
. Then, we compare the coefficients between (
3.5
) and (
3.4
) to obtain that

(3.6)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\lambda_{1}+2\lambda_{2}=-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right):=S_{1},\\
&2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}=\eta^{2}pp_{e}+\frac{\eta^{4}\kappa\mu\theta_{e}}{v^{2}}-\eta^{2}p_{v}:=S_{2},\\
&\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{2}=\frac{\eta^{4}\kappa\theta_{e}p_{v}}{v}:=S_{3}.\end{aligned}\right.

We multiply
2\lambda_{2}
to the first equation and minus the second equation to obtain an equation for
\lambda_{2}
as below,

\lambda_{2}^{2}=\frac{2\lambda_{2}S_{1}-S_{2}}{3}.

The above equality is a reduction from the second order term
\lambda_{2}^{2}
to first order and zero order term. Then we can substitute this reduction to (
3.4
) and
(
3.6
)
to solve
\lambda_{2}
respectively. Actually, we have the following two representation of
\lambda_{2}
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}=\frac{S_{1}S_{2}-9S_{3}}{2S_{1}^{2}+3S_{1}-9S_{2}},

\displaystyle\quad\text{from \eqref{char}},

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}=\frac{S_{1}S_{2}-9S_{3}}{2\left(S_{1}^{2}-3S_{2}\right)},

\displaystyle\quad\text{from $\eqref{B5}_{2}$ and $\eqref{B5}_{3}$}.

Then the two representation should coincide if
\lambda_{2}
is the root of (
3.4
). As we already know
\lambda_{2}\neq 0
, we immediately have
S_{1}=S_{2}
if the two representation of
\lambda_{2}
are equal. Then we solve the equation
S_{1}=S_{2}
to obtain

\eta=0,\quad\eta=-\frac{i\sqrt{v}\sqrt{\kappa\theta_{e}+pvp_{e}+\mu-vp_{v}}}{\sqrt{\kappa}\sqrt{\mu}\sqrt{\theta_{e}}},\quad\eta=\frac{i\sqrt{v}\sqrt{\kappa\theta_{e}+pvp_{e}+\mu-vp_{v}}}{\sqrt{\kappa}\sqrt{\mu}\sqrt{\theta_{e}}}.

Therefore, the multiple root cannot occur unless when
\eta
take above values. This means when
|\eta|>0
and
|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}
for sufficiently small positive constant
\sigma_{0}
, the matrix
\left(-i\eta F^{\prime}(\bar{U})-\eta^{2}B(\bar{U})\right)
admits three distinct eigenvalues.

∎

Once we know the eigenvalues of the matrix
\left(-i\eta F^{\prime}(\bar{U})-\eta^{2}B(\bar{U})\right)
, we can solve the linear system (
3.2
) via the resolution. In fact, according to (
3.2
), we have

(3.7)

\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\eta,t;\bar{U})=\mathcal{L}^{-1}[\left(s+i\eta F^{\prime}(\bar{U})+\eta^{2}B(\bar{U})\right)^{-1}],

where
\mathcal{L}^{-1}
is the inverse Laplace transform with respect to
s
. On the other hand, according to Lemma
3.1
, the eigenvalues are distinct near the real line. Therefore,
\left(s+i\eta F^{\prime}(\bar{U})+\eta^{2}B(\bar{U})\right)^{-1}
has three simple poles when
\eta
near the real line, which guarantees the inverse Laplace transform (
3.7
) has the following representation,

(3.8)

\mathcal{L}^{-1}[\left(s+i\eta F^{\prime}(\bar{U})+\eta^{2}B(\bar{U})\right)^{-1}]=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\text{Res}[e^{st}\left(s+i\eta F^{\prime}(\bar{U})+\eta^{2}B(\bar{U})\right)^{-1},\lambda_{j}],

where
\text{Res}[f,z_{0}]
denotes the residue of function
f
at the pole
z_{0}
. More precisely, we can combine (
3.7
) and (
3.8
) to obtain the representation of the Green’s function in frequency space as follows,

(3.9)

\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\eta,t;\bar{U})=\sum_{j=1}^{3}e^{\lambda_{j}t}\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j},\quad\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}=\frac{\text{adj}\left(s+i\eta F^{\prime}(\bar{U})+\eta^{2}B(\bar{U})\right)|_{s=\lambda_{j}}}{\Pi_{k\neq j}(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k})},

where
\text{adj}(A)
is the adjugate matrix of
A
.

3.1.2.
Spectral gap

Next, we will show there exists a spectral gap of the eigenvalues for
\eta
on the real axis and away from
0
, which will be very important for time decay estimates later. We are motivated by the idea in
[
12
]
to prove the following Lemma.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
r
and
R
be two positive constants, we can find a positive constant
b
such that
Re\left(\lambda_{j}(\eta)\right)\leq-b
for all real number
\eta
satisfying
0<r<|\eta|<R
,
j=1,2,3
.

Proof.

The characteristic polynomial (
3.4
) can be rewritten as follows,

(3.10)

f(\lambda)=\lambda^{3}+\lambda^{2}\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)+\lambda\left(\eta^{2}pp_{e}+\frac{\eta^{4}\kappa\mu\theta_{e}}{v^{2}}-\eta^{2}p_{v}\right)-\frac{\eta^{4}\kappa\theta_{e}p_{v}}{v}.

Then we let
b_{1}
be a positive constant such that
0<b_{1}<\min\left\{1,\frac{\kappa p_{v}\theta_{e}r^{4}v}{v^{2}(pp_{e}-p_{v})R^{2}+R^{4}\kappa\mu\theta_{e}+v^{2}}\right\}
, and prove all real roots of (
3.4
) are less than
-b_{1}
. In fact, for
\lambda\geq b_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle f(\lambda)

\displaystyle\geq-b_{1}^{2}-b_{1}\left(\eta^{2}pp_{e}+\frac{\eta^{4}\kappa\mu\theta_{e}}{v^{2}}-\eta^{2}p_{v}\right)-\frac{\eta^{4}\kappa\theta_{e}p_{v}}{v}

\displaystyle\geq-b_{1}\left(1+\eta^{2}pp_{e}+\frac{\eta^{4}\kappa\mu\theta_{e}}{v^{2}}-\eta^{2}p_{v}\right)-\frac{\eta^{4}\kappa\theta_{e}p_{v}}{v}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{r^{4}\kappa\theta_{e}p_{v}}{v}-\frac{\eta^{4}\kappa\theta_{e}p_{v}}{v}

\displaystyle>0,

where the last inequality holds due to
r<|\eta|
and
p_{v}<0
, which is according to (
1.2
). Now, if (
3.4
) admits three real roots, then all the real roots must be less than
-b_{1}
. Otherwise, (
3.4
) will admit one real root and two congugate complex roots. The real root will be less than
-b_{1}
, and thus we only need to deal with the two conjugate roots
\lambda_{2}
and
\lambda_{3}
. In fact, we have the following relations,

Re(\lambda_{2})=Re(\lambda_{3}),\quad 2Re(\lambda_{2})=\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}=-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)-\lambda_{1}.

Thus, in order to prove
Re(\lambda_{2})
is negative, we need to show the negative real root
\lambda_{1}
is close to zero. To this end, we use Taylor expansion to rewrite (
3.10
) as follows,

\displaystyle f(\lambda)

\displaystyle=f\left(-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)\right)+f^{\prime}\left(-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)\right)\left(\lambda+\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}\left(-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)\right)}{2}\left(\lambda+\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)\right)^{2}+\left(\lambda+\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)\right)^{3},

where the expansion is finite because
f^{\prime\prime\prime}=6
and the higher order derivative vanishes. By direct calculation, we have the following estimates,

\displaystyle f\left(-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)\right)=-\frac{\eta^{4}\left(\eta^{2}\kappa\mu\theta_{e}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}+\mu\right)+pv^{2}p_{e}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}+\mu\right)-\mu v^{2}p_{v}\right)}{v^{3}}\leq-\frac{r^{6}\kappa\mu\theta_{e}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}+\mu\right)}{v^{3}},

\displaystyle f^{\prime}\left(-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)\right)=\eta^{2}\left(pp_{e}+\frac{\eta^{2}\left(\kappa^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}+3\kappa\mu\theta_{e}+\mu^{2}\right)}{v^{2}}-p_{v}\right)\leq R^{2}\left(pp_{e}+\frac{R^{2}\left(\kappa^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}+3\kappa\mu\theta_{e}+\mu^{2}\right)}{v^{2}}-p_{v}\right),

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}\left(-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)\right)=-\frac{4\eta^{2}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}+\mu\right)}{v}<0.

Now we let
b_{2}
be a positive constant such that
0<b_{2}<\min\left\{1,\frac{r^{6}\kappa\mu\theta_{e}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}+\mu\right)}{v^{3}\left((pp_{e}-p_{v})R^{2}+\frac{R^{4}\left(\kappa^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}+3\kappa\mu\theta_{e}+\mu^{2}\right)}{v^{2}}+1\right)}\right\}
. Then, for
-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)\leq x\leq-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)+b_{2}
, we can substitute above estimates of
f
,
f^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime\prime}
into the Taylor expansion and obtain the following estimates,

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{r^{6}\kappa\mu\theta_{e}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}+\mu\right)}{v^{3}}+R^{2}\left(pp_{e}+\frac{R^{2}\left(\kappa^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}+3\kappa\mu\theta_{e}+\mu^{2}\right)}{v^{2}}-p_{v}\right)b_{2}+b_{2}^{3}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{r^{6}\kappa\mu\theta_{e}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}+\mu\right)}{v^{3}}+\left((pp_{e}-p_{v})R^{2}+\frac{R^{4}\left(\kappa^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}+3\kappa\mu\theta_{e}+\mu^{2}\right)}{v^{2}}+1\right)b_{2}<0.

Thus, the real root
\lambda_{1}
must be less than
-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)
or larger than
-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)+b_{2}
. On the other hand, we already know that
f(\lambda)>0
for all real
\lambda>-b_{1}
, therefore, we have

f\left(-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)+b_{2}\right)<0,\quad f(b_{1}+b_{2})>0,\quad-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)+b_{2}<b_{1}+b_{2}.

Thus, we apply the continuity of
f(\lambda)
to conclude there is a real root between
\left(-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)+b_{2}\right)
and
b_{1}+b_{2}
. Moreover, as we assume there are two conjugate roots
\lambda_{2}
and
\lambda_{3}
, we have
\lambda_{1}>\left(-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)+b_{2}\right)
, which immediately implies that

Re(\lambda_{2})=Re(\lambda_{3})=\frac{-\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}\right)-\lambda_{1}}{2}<-b_{2}.

Finally, we let
b=\min\left\{b_{1},b_{2}\right\}
and finish the proof.

∎

3.1.3.
Eigenvalue at infinity

We assume
\lambda
to be a Laurent polynomial of
\eta
with coefficients undetermined, and substitute the polynomial in (
3.4
) to solve all the coefficients and obtain the following three asymptotic expansion when
\eta
tends to
+\infty
,

(3.11)

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{vp_{v}}{\mu}-\frac{v^{3}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}p_{v}^{2}+\mu pp_{e}p_{v}\right)}{\kappa\mu^{3}\theta_{e}}\eta^{-2}+\frac{v^{5}p_{v}\left(\mu^{2}p^{2}p_{e}^{2}+2\kappa^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}p_{v}^{2}+\mu pp_{e}p_{v}\left(3\kappa\theta_{e}+\mu\right)\right)}{\kappa^{2}\mu^{5}\theta_{e}^{2}}\eta^{-4}

\displaystyle-\frac{v^{7}p_{v}\left(\mu^{3}p^{3}p_{e}^{3}+3\mu^{2}p^{2}p_{e}^{2}p_{v}\left(2\kappa\theta_{e}+\mu\right)+5\kappa^{3}\theta_{e}^{3}p_{v}^{3}+\mu pp_{e}p_{v}^{2}\left(10\kappa^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}+4\kappa\mu\theta_{e}+\mu^{2}\right)\right)}{\kappa^{3}\mu^{7}\theta_{e}^{3}}\eta^{-6}+O(1)\eta^{-8},

(3.12)

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\eta^{2}\mu}{v}+\frac{v\left(\mu pp_{e}+\kappa\theta_{e}p_{v}-\mu p_{v}\right)}{\mu\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right)}+\eta^{-2}\left(\frac{v^{3}\left(\mu^{2}pp_{e}-p_{v}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{2}\right)\left(\mu pp_{e}+p_{v}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}-\mu\right)\right)}{\mu^{3}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{3}}\right)

\displaystyle+\eta^{-4}\left(\frac{v^{5}\left(2\mu^{5}p^{3}p_{e}^{3}+\mu^{2}p^{2}p_{e}^{2}p_{v}\left(\kappa^{3}\theta_{e}^{2}-5\kappa^{2}\mu\theta_{e}^{2}+10\kappa\mu^{2}\theta_{e}-6\mu^{3}\right)\right)}{\mu^{5}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{5}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{v^{5}\left(\mu pp_{e}p_{v}^{2}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{2}\left(3\kappa^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}-8\kappa\mu\theta_{e}+6\mu^{2}\right)-2p_{v}^{3}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{5}\right)}{\mu^{5}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right)^{5}}\right)

\displaystyle+\eta^{-6}\left(\frac{v^{7}\left(5\mu^{7}p^{4}p_{e}^{3}-\mu^{3}p^{3}p_{e}^{3}p_{v}\left(\kappa^{4}\theta_{e}^{3}-7\kappa^{3}\mu\theta_{e}^{3}+21\kappa^{2}\mu^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}-35\kappa\mu^{3}\theta_{e}+20\mu^{4}\right)\right)}{\mu^{7}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{7}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{v^{7}\left(3\mu^{2}p^{2}p_{e}^{2}p_{v}^{2}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{2}\left(-2\kappa^{3}\theta_{e}^{3}+9\kappa^{2}\mu\theta_{e}^{2}-15\kappa\mu^{2}\theta_{e}+10\mu^{3}\right)\right)}{\mu^{7}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{7}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{v^{7}\left(\mu pp_{e}p_{v}^{3}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{3}\left(-10\kappa^{3}\theta_{e}^{3}+36\kappa^{2}\mu\theta_{e}^{2}-45\kappa\mu^{2}\theta_{e}+20\mu^{3}\right)+5p_{v}^{4}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{7}\right)}{\mu^{7}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{7}}\right)+O(1)\eta^{-8},

(3.13)

\displaystyle\lambda_{3}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}}{v}+\frac{pvp_{e}}{\kappa\theta_{e}-\mu}+\eta^{-2}\left(\frac{pv^{3}p_{e}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}\left(pp_{e}+p_{v}\right)-\mu p_{v}\right)}{\kappa\theta_{e}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}-\mu\right){}^{3}}\right)

\displaystyle+\eta^{-4}\left(\frac{pv^{5}p_{e}\left(2\kappa^{2}p^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}p_{e}^{2}+pp_{e}p_{v}\left(4\kappa^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}-5\kappa\mu\theta_{e}+\mu^{2}\right)+p_{v}^{2}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{2}\right)}{\kappa^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}-\mu\right){}^{5}}\right)

\displaystyle+\eta^{-6}\left(\frac{pv^{7}p_{e}\left(5\kappa^{3}p^{3}\theta_{e}^{3}p_{e}^{2}-p^{2}p_{e}^{2}p_{v}\left(-15\kappa^{3}\theta_{e}^{3}+21\kappa^{2}\mu\theta_{e}^{2}-7\kappa\mu^{2}\theta_{e}+\mu^{3}\right)-3pp_{e}p_{v}^{2}\left(\mu-3\kappa\theta_{e}\right)\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{2}\right)}{\kappa^{3}\theta_{e}^{3}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}-\mu\right){}^{7}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{pv^{7}p_{e}\left(p_{v}^{3}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{3}\right)}{\eta^{6}\kappa^{3}\theta_{e}^{3}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}-\mu\right){}^{7}}\right)+O(1)\eta^{-8}.

Morevover, as we consider the ideal gas, the Prandtl number is usually smaller than one. Then we combine the constitutive relation (
1.2
) and the definition of Prandtl number Pr to obtain

\frac{\mu}{\kappa\theta_{e}}=\frac{\mu c_{v}}{\kappa}=\text{Pr}\frac{c_{v}}{c_{p}}<1.

Therefore, the denominators in the expansions above are nonzero and thus the formulae are well-defined. Moreover, when
\eta
tends to infinity, the quadratic term would be dominant in the expansion of eigenvalues, and we can use above inequality to conclude

(3.14)

0>\text{Re}(\lambda_{1})>\text{Re}(\lambda_{2})>\text{Re}(\lambda_{3}),\quad\eta\rightarrow\infty.

3.1.4.
Eigenvalue at zero

Now we switch to study the asymptotic behavior of the three eigenvalues when
\eta
near zero. Similar as the expansion of eigenvalues at infinity, we can assume the eigenvalues to be polynomials of
\eta
and substitute them into (
3.4
) to obtain the following expansions when
\eta
tens to zero,

(3.15)

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{\eta^{2}\kappa\theta_{e}p_{v}}{v\left(pp_{e}-p_{v}\right)}-\frac{\eta^{4}\kappa^{2}p\theta_{e}^{2}p_{e}p_{v}\left(\mu pp_{e}+p_{v}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}-\mu\right)\right)}{v^{3}\left(p_{v}-pp_{e}\right){}^{4}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\eta^{6}\kappa^{3}p\theta_{e}^{3}p_{e}p_{v}\left(\mu^{2}\left(-p^{3}\right)p_{e}^{3}+\mu p^{2}p_{e}^{2}p_{v}\left(\mu-3\kappa\theta_{e}\right)+pp_{e}p_{v}^{2}\left(-2\kappa^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}+\kappa\mu\theta_{e}+\mu^{2}\right)-p_{v}^{3}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{2}\right)}{v^{5}\left(pp_{e}-p_{v}\right){}^{7}}+O(1)\eta^{6},

(3.16)

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}=

\displaystyle-i\eta\sqrt{pp_{e}-p_{v}}-\frac{\eta^{2}\left(\kappa p\theta_{e}p_{e}+\mu pp_{e}-\mu p_{v}\right)}{2v\left(pp_{e}-p_{v}\right)}-\frac{i\eta^{3}\left(-p^{2}p_{e}^{2}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{2}+2pp_{e}p_{v}\left(2\kappa^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}-\kappa\mu\theta_{e}+\mu^{2}\right)-\mu^{2}p_{v}^{2}\right)}{8v^{2}\left(pp_{e}-p_{v}\right){}^{5/2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\eta^{4}\kappa^{2}p\theta_{e}^{2}p_{e}p_{v}\left(\mu pp_{e}+p_{v}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}-\mu\right)\right)}{2v^{3}\left(p_{v}-pp_{e}\right){}^{4}}+O(1)\eta^{4},

(3.17)

\displaystyle\lambda_{3}=

\displaystyle i\eta\sqrt{pp_{e}-p_{v}}-\frac{\eta^{2}\left(\kappa p\theta_{e}p_{e}+\mu pp_{e}-\mu p_{v}\right)}{2v\left(pp_{e}-p_{v}\right)}-\frac{i\eta^{3}\left(p^{2}p_{e}^{2}\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right){}^{2}-2pp_{e}p_{v}\left(2\kappa^{2}\theta_{e}^{2}-\kappa\mu\theta_{e}+\mu^{2}\right)+\mu^{2}p_{v}^{2}\right)}{8v^{2}\left(pp_{e}-p_{v}\right){}^{5/2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\eta^{4}\kappa^{2}p\theta_{e}^{2}p_{e}p_{v}\left(\mu pp_{e}+p_{v}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}-\mu\right)\right)}{2v^{3}\left(p_{v}-pp_{e}\right){}^{4}}+O(1)\eta^{4}.

Note the coefficients of
i\eta
in
\lambda_{i}
are
\pm\sqrt{pp_{e}-p_{v}}
and
0
respectively, which are actually the eigenvalues of the matrix
F^{\prime}(\bar{U})
and corresponding to the sound speed. On the other hand, according to (
1.2
),
p_{e}>0
and
p_{v}<0
, therefore the coefficients of
\eta^{2}
in all
\lambda_{i}
are negative, which correspond to the combined effect of viscosity and heat conductivity and represent the dissipative structure of the system.

3.2.
Singular parts of Green’s function

In this part, we will treat the singular part of Green’s function. More precisely, in order to obtain the space time structure of the Green’s function, we need inverse Fourier transform of
\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\eta,t;\bar{U})
. Following the methodology in
[
15
]
, the first step is to study the high frequency part of
\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\eta,t;\bar{U})
and capture the singularities.

3.2.1.
Approximated eigenvalue

For high frequency, we note the constant terms in the expansion of the eigenvalues at infinity. This relates to the appearance of Dirac-delta function. However, we can not directly use the formula of
\lambda_{j}
and
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}
to construct the space time Green’s function. This is because the inverse power of
\eta
in (
3.11
), (
3.12
) and (
3.13
) makes the expansion not analytic at zero, which is difficult to apply the complex contour integral to analyze the inverse Fourier transform. Therefore, for convenience in later analysis, we will use another approximate eigenvalues as below,

(3.18)

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{*}=\beta_{1}^{*}+\sum_{k=1}^{3}\frac{A_{1,k}}{(1+\eta^{2})^{k}}-\frac{K_{1}}{\left(\eta^{2}+1\right)^{4}},

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}^{*}=-\alpha^{*}_{2}\eta^{2}+\beta^{*}_{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{3}\frac{A_{2,k}}{(1+\eta^{2})^{k}}-\frac{K_{2}}{\left(\eta^{2}+1\right)^{4}},

\displaystyle\lambda_{3}^{*}=-\alpha^{*}_{3}\eta^{2}+\beta^{*}_{3}+\sum_{k=1}^{3}\frac{A_{3,k}}{(1+\eta^{2})^{k}}-\frac{K_{3}}{\left(\eta^{2}+1\right)^{4}},

where the coefficients
\alpha^{*}_{j}
,
\beta^{*}_{j}
and
A_{j,k}
are listed in Appendix
A
, and
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
and
K_{3}
are large enough positive constants to guarantee the negative sign of the real part of the approximate eigenvalues. Now we have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

For a fixed state
\bar{U}
, we can find positive constants
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
,
K_{3}
,
\sigma_{0}
,
\sigma_{0}^{*}
and
\sigma_{1}^{*}
such that the following asserts hold:

(1)

The approximate eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}^{*}
are analytic around the real axis. More precisely,
\lambda_{j}^{*}
is analytic for
j=1,2,3
in the following region,

\{\eta:\ |Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}\}.

(2)

\lambda_{j}^{*}
is an approximation of
\lambda_{j}
which is accurate up to the power
\left(\eta^{2}+1\right)^{-3}
, and thus we have

(3.19)

\lambda_{j}^{*}=\lambda_{j}+O(1)|\eta|^{-8}.

(3)

In the region
\{\eta:\ |Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}\}
, all the three approximated eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}^{*}
have negative real part and are distinct. Actually, we have

\sup_{|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}}Re(\lambda_{1}^{*})<-\sigma_{0}^{*},\qquad\qquad\sup_{|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}}Re(\lambda_{2}^{*}+\frac{\mu}{2v}(\eta^{2}+1))<-\sigma_{0}^{*},

\sup_{|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}}Re(\lambda_{3}^{*}+\frac{\kappa\theta_{e}}{2v}(\eta^{2}+1))<-\sigma_{0}^{*},\qquad\quad\min_{j,k}\inf_{|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}}|Re(\lambda_{k}^{*}-\lambda_{j}^{*})|=\sigma_{1}^{*}.

Proof.

At the fixed state
\bar{U}
, all the coefficients in
\lambda_{j}^{*}
are constants.

(1)

For the first assertion, we can let
\sigma_{0}
sufficiently small so that the imaginary part of
\eta
is very small and
1+\eta^{2}
has a positive lower bound. Therefore, we immediately have
\lambda_{j}^{*}
are analytic for
j=1,2,3
.

(2)

The first four terms of
\lambda_{j}^{*}
is from the expansion of
\lambda_{j}
at infinity, therefore we have

|\lambda_{j}^{*}+\frac{K_{0}}{\left(\eta^{2}+1\right)^{4}}-\lambda_{j}|=O(1)|\eta|^{-8}\Longrightarrow\lambda_{j}^{*}=\lambda_{j}+O(1)|\eta|^{-8}.

(3)

When
\eta
tends to infinity, the leading term would be dominant. Then we can apply (
3.14
) to find a positive constant
\sigma_{0}^{*}
and a sufficiently large constant
C_{1}
such that

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}\\
|\eta|>C_{1}\end{subarray}}Re(\lambda_{1}^{*})<-\sigma_{0}^{*}.

For
|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}
and
|\eta|\leq C_{1}
, we know the first four terms of
\lambda_{1}^{*}
are bounded. Then, we can make
K_{1}
sufficiently large to guarantee the negative sign of
\lambda_{1}^{*}
. Therefore, we have for sufficiently large
K_{1}
that

\sup_{|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}}Re(\lambda_{1}^{*})<-\sigma_{0}^{*}.

Similar arguments can be applied to prove the negative sign of the real part of
\lambda_{2}^{*}
and
\lambda_{3}^{*}
. Moreover, for
|\eta|\leq C_{1}
we can choose
K_{2},K_{3}
be sufficiently large and satisfy
K_{1}<K_{2}<K_{3}
. Then we have

0>Re(\lambda_{1}^{*})>Re(\lambda_{2}^{*})>Re(\lambda_{3}^{*}),\quad|\eta|\leq C_{1}.

On the other hand, when
|\eta|>C_{1}
, we apply (
3.14
) and the fact
K_{1}<K_{2}<K_{3}
to have

0>Re(\lambda_{1}^{*})>Re(\lambda_{2}^{*})>Re(\lambda_{3}^{*}),\quad|\eta|>C_{1}.

Thus, we can conclude that
\lambda_{j}^{*}
are distinct for all
\eta
and we can find a positive constant
\sigma_{1}^{*}
such that

\min_{j,k}\inf_{|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}}|Re(\lambda_{k}^{*}-\lambda_{j}^{*})|=\sigma_{1}^{*}.

This finish the proof of the proposition.

∎

3.2.2.
Representation of singular part

Now we are going to construct the singular part of the Green’s function. According to (
3.9
), we can express the Green’s function in terms of eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}
for sufficiently large
\eta
. Actually we have the expression of
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}
as follows when
\eta
near infinity,

(3.20)

\frac{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}pp_{e}\eta^{2}+\frac{\left(\mu\eta^{2}+v\lambda_{j}\right)\left(\kappa\theta_{e}\eta^{2}+v\lambda_{j}\right)}{v^{2}}&\frac{i\eta\left(\kappa\theta_{e}\eta^{2}+iuvp_{e}\eta+v\lambda_{j}\right)}{v}&\eta^{2}p_{e}\\
-\frac{i\eta p_{v}\left(\kappa\theta_{e}\eta^{2}+v\lambda_{j}\right)}{v}&\lambda_{j}\left(\frac{\kappa\theta_{e}\eta^{2}}{v}+iup_{e}\eta+\lambda_{j}\right)&-i\eta\lambda_{j}p_{e}\\
-\frac{\eta p_{v}\left(iu\kappa\theta_{e}\eta^{2}+pv\eta+iuv\lambda_{j}\right)}{v}&\frac{\eta\left(iv\lambda_{j}p_{e}u^{2}+v\eta p_{v}u+\lambda_{j}\left(-ipv-u\eta\mu+u\eta\kappa\theta_{e}\right)\right)}{v}&-p_{v}\eta^{2}-iu\lambda_{j}p_{e}\eta+\lambda_{j}\left(\frac{\mu\eta^{2}}{v}+\lambda_{j}\right)\\
\end{array}\right)}{\Pi_{k\neq j}(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k})}.

Note
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}
can be viewed as a matrix valued function
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}(\eta;\lambda)
, which may not be analytic. We replace
\lambda_{j}
by the approximate eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}^{*}
to approximate
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}(\eta;\lambda)
for high frequency, then it turns out that
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}(\eta;\lambda_{j}^{*})
is analytic around the real axis in the following lemma. In the following, we will denote
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}(\eta;\lambda_{j}^{*})
by
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}^{*}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

The matrix
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}^{*}
is analytic in the region
|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}
around the real axis, and it has the following expansion at infinity,

(3.21)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}^{*}=M_{j}^{*,0}+i\eta^{-1}M_{j}^{*,1}+\eta^{-2}M_{j}^{*,2}+i\eta^{-3}M_{j}^{*,3}+\eta^{-4}M_{j}^{*,4}+O(1)\eta^{-5},\quad j=1,2,3,\quad|\eta|\rightarrow\infty.

where
M_{j}^{*,k}
are listed in (
B.1
), (
B.2
) and (
B.3
) in Appendix
B
.

Proof.

According to Lemma
3.3
,
\lambda_{j}^{*}
are distinct and there is an uniform gap between two approximate eigenvalues. Therefore, according to (
3.20
), the denominator of
\mathbb{M}_{j}^{*}
are nonzero so that
\mathbb{M}_{j}^{*}
are well-defined for
j=1,2,3
. Then the analyticity of
\lambda_{j}^{*}
immediately implies the analyticity of
\mathbb{M}_{j}^{*}
in the region
|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}
. Finally, the expansion of
\mathbb{M}_{j}^{*}
can be obtained by direct calculations.
∎

Now we substitute
\lambda_{j}^{*}
and
\mathbb{M}_{j}^{*}
into the representation of Green’s function (
3.9
) to obtain the high frequency approximation,

(3.22)

\mathbb{G}^{*}(x,t;\bar{U})=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{*}(\eta,t;\bar{U})\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{*,j}\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{3}G^{*,j}(x,t;\bar{U}),\quad\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{*,j}=e^{\lambda_{j}^{*}t}\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}^{*},\quad j=1,2,3,

which will be shown as the singular part of the Green’s function. In the rest of this subsections, we will do inverse Fourier transform to (
3.22
) to find the space-time structure of singular part
\mathbb{G}^{*}(x,t;\bar{U})
.

Remark 3.1
.

Note in (
3.9
), we have the representation of the Green’s function in Fourier space as follows,

\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\eta,t;\bar{U})=\sum_{j=1}^{3}e^{\lambda_{j}t}\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j},\quad\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}=\frac{\text{adj}\left(s+i\eta F^{\prime}(\bar{U})+\eta^{2}B(\bar{U})\right)|_{s=\lambda_{j}}}{\Pi_{k\neq j}(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k})}.

Moreover, from (
3.2
), we know the initial data
\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\eta,0;\bar{U})=I
, which is independent of
\eta
and
\lambda_{j}
due to the cancelations in
\sum_{j=1}^{3}\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}(\eta;\lambda_{j})
. On the other hand, according to (
3.22
), the construction of the singular part of Green’s function is just replacing
\lambda_{j}
by
\lambda_{j}^{*}
in (
3.9
). Therefore, when we take
t=0
, we have

\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{*}(\eta,0;\bar{U})=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{*,j}(\eta,0;\bar{U})=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}(\eta;\lambda_{j}^{*})=I.

The above formula shows the singular part
\mathbb{G}^{*}
absorbs all initial data of the Green’s function
\mathbb{G}
.

3.2.3.
Pointwise estimate of
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}^{*}]
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[e^{\lambda_{j}^{*}t}]

In this part, we will calculate the inverse Fourier transform of
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}^{*}
and
e^{\lambda_{j}^{*}t}
, which will lead to the space-time pointwise estimates of
\mathbb{G}^{*}(x,t;\bar{U})
. We start with the following preliminary lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

[
15
]
Let
\hat{f}(\eta)
be the Fourier transform of
f(x)
. Suppose, in the region
|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}
, we have
\hat{f}(\eta)
is analytic and
\hat{f}(\eta)
has the following asymptotic property,

(3.23)

\hat{f}(\eta)=O(1)\frac{\eta}{(1+|\eta|)^{(2m+2)}}+O(1)\frac{1}{(1+|\eta|)^{(2m+2)}},\quad m\geq 0,\quad\eta\rightarrow\infty.

Then
f(x)\in H^{2m}(\mathbb{R})
and

\sum_{j=0}^{2m}|\frac{d^{j}}{dx^{j}}f(x)|=O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}|x|},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

By Plancherel’s Theorem of Fourier transform, the assertion
f(x)\in H^{2m}(\mathbb{R})
is equivalent to
\eta^{j}\hat{f}(\eta)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
for
0\leq j\leq 2m
. As
\hat{f}
is analytic in the region
Im(\eta)<\sigma_{0}
and thus has no singularity on the real axis, we only need check for the highest order
2m
. In fact, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\frac{d^{2m}}{dx^{2m}}f(x)|^{2}dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|(i\eta)^{2m}\hat{f}(\eta)|^{2}d\eta\leq O(1)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\eta|^{4m+2}}{(1+|\eta|)^{4m+4}}d\eta\leq O(1),

which shows
f(x)\in H^{2m}(\mathbb{R})
. For the second assertion, we only consider the case
x>0
, and the other case can be similarly treated. As
\hat{f}(\eta)
is analytic in the region
|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}
, we can apply Cauchy contour Theorem to shift the integral path of the inverse Fourier transform, and apply the decay property of
\hat{f}(\eta)
to have the following estimate for
0\leq j\leq 2m
,

\displaystyle|\frac{d^{j}}{dx^{j}}f(x)|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\eta x}(i\eta)^{j}\hat{f}(\eta)d\eta\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(\eta+i\sigma_{0})x}(i\eta+i\sigma_{0})^{j}\hat{f}(\eta+i\sigma_{0})d\eta\right|

\displaystyle=e^{-\sigma_{0}x}\left|\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\eta x}(i\eta+i\sigma_{0})^{j}\hat{f}(\eta+i\sigma_{0})d\eta\right|.

Now, in the region
|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}
, if
|\eta|>C
for some large positive constant
C
, we have the asymptotic property (
3.23
). Moreover, for other
\eta
in the region
|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}
such that
|\eta|\leq C
, it is obvious that
(i\eta+i\sigma_{0})^{j}\hat{f}(\eta+i\sigma_{0})
will be uniformly bounded due to the analyticity of
\hat{f}
. Therefore, we have the following estimate for the inverse Fourier transform,

\displaystyle|\frac{d^{j}}{dx^{j}}f(x)|f

\displaystyle=e^{-\sigma_{0}x}\left|\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{|\eta|\leq C}e^{i\eta x}(i\eta+i\sigma_{0})^{j}\hat{f}(\eta+i\sigma_{0})d\eta+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{|\eta|>C}e^{i\eta x}(i\eta+i\sigma_{0})^{j}\hat{f}(\eta+i\sigma_{0})d\eta\right|

\displaystyle=e^{-\sigma_{0}x}\left|O(1)+\frac{O(1)}{2\pi}\int_{|\eta|>C}\frac{e^{i\eta x}(i\eta+i\sigma_{0})^{j}(\eta+i\sigma_{0})}{(1+|\eta+i\sigma_{0}|)^{2m+2}}d\eta+\frac{O(1)}{2\pi}\int_{|\eta|>C}\frac{e^{i\eta x}(i\eta+i\sigma_{0})^{j}}{(1+|\eta+i\sigma_{0}|)^{2m+2}}d\eta\right|

\displaystyle=e^{-\sigma_{0}x}\left|\frac{O(1)}{2\pi}\int_{|\eta|>C}\frac{e^{i\eta x}}{(1+|\eta|)}d\eta+\frac{O(1)}{2\pi}\int_{|\eta|>C}\frac{e^{i\eta x}}{(1+|\eta|)^{2}}d\eta\right|

\displaystyle=O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}x},

where the last equality comes from the integrability of the function
\frac{sinx}{x}
. Then, we add up above estimates for all
j
to verify the second assertion, and finish the proof.
∎

Now, we are ready to provide the pointwise space-time estimate of
\mathbb{M}_{j}^{*}(x)
. Actually, in the following lemma, we can combine Lemma
3.5
and the expansion of
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}^{*}
to obtain the estimates up to fourth order derivative of
\mathbb{M}_{j}^{*}(x)
.

Lemma 3.6
.

For the positive constant
\sigma_{0}
defined in Lemma
3.3
and Lemma
3.4
, we have the following pointwise estimates for
\mathbb{M}_{j}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{j}^{*}(x)=\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,0}+O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}|x|},

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\mathbb{M}_{j}^{*}(x)=\frac{d}{dx}\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,0}-\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,1}+O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}|x|},

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}\mathbb{M}_{j}^{*}(x)=\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,0}-\frac{d}{dx}\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,1}+\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,2}+O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}|x|},

\displaystyle\frac{d^{3}}{dx^{3}}\mathbb{M}_{j}^{*}(x)=\frac{d^{3}}{dx^{3}}\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,0}-\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,1}+\frac{d}{dx}\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,2}+\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,3}+O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}|x|},

\displaystyle\frac{d^{4}}{dx^{4}}\mathbb{M}_{j}^{*}(x)=\frac{d^{4}}{dx^{4}}\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,0}-\frac{d^{3}}{dx^{3}}\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,1}+\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,2}+\frac{d}{dx}\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,3}+\delta(x)M_{j}^{*,4}+O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}|x|}.

Proof.

We will only prove the zeroth order estimate of
\mathbb{M}_{1}^{*}(x)
, and the other estimates can be similarly obtained. According to Lemma
3.4
,
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{1}^{*}
is analytic in the region
|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}
and has the expansion (
3.21
), therefore we have
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{1}^{*}-M_{1}^{*,0}
is also analytic in the region
|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}
. Moreover, we have

\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{1}^{*}-M_{1}^{*,0}=O(1)i\eta^{-1}+O(1)\eta^{-2},\quad\eta\rightarrow\infty,

where
O(1)
denotes an order one matrix in above formula. Thus we have
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{1}^{*}-M_{1}^{*,0}
satisfies the requirements in Lemma
3.5
with
m=0
. Therefore, we apply Lemma
3.5
to obtain

|\mathbb{M}_{1}^{*}(x)-\delta(x)M_{1}^{*,0}|=O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}|x|},

which finish the proof of the zeroth order estimate of
\mathbb{M}_{1}^{*}(x)
. The others can be obtained in the same way.
∎

Next, we will study the inverse Fourier transform of
e^{\lambda_{j}^{*}t}
. It is a little bit different from the inverse of
\hat{\mathbb{M}}_{j}^{*}
, since the leading order of
\lambda_{j}^{*}
are different. Let
\Lambda_{j}^{*}(x,t)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[e^{\lambda_{j}^{*}t}\right]
, we have the following estimates.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\sigma_{0}
and
\sigma_{0}^{*}
be positive constants defined in Lemma
3.3
and Lemma
3.4
, then we have the following point-wise estimates for
\Lambda_{j}^{*}(x,t)
up to the third order,

(3.24)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Lambda_{1}^{*}(x,t)=e^{\frac{vp_{v}}{\mu}t}\delta(x)+\left\{\begin{aligned} &O(1)te^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},\quad 0<t<1,\\
&O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},\quad t\geq 1,\end{aligned}\right.\\
&\partial_{x}\Lambda_{1}^{*}(x,t)=e^{\frac{vp_{v}}{\mu}t}\frac{d}{dx}\delta(x)+\left\{\begin{aligned} &O(1)te^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},\quad 0<t<1,\\
&O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},\quad t\geq 1,\end{aligned}\right.\\
&\partial_{x}^{2}\Lambda_{1}^{*}(x,t)=e^{\frac{vp_{v}}{\mu}t}\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}\delta(x)-A_{1,1}te^{\frac{vp_{v}}{\mu}t}\delta(x)+\left\{\begin{aligned} &O(1)te^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},\quad 0<t<1,\\
&O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},\quad t\geq 1,\end{aligned}\right.\\
&\partial_{x}^{3}\Lambda_{1}^{*}(x,t)=e^{\frac{vp_{v}}{\mu}t}\frac{d^{3}}{dx^{3}}\delta(x)-A_{1,1}te^{\frac{vp_{v}}{\mu}t}\frac{d}{dx}\delta(x)+\left\{\begin{aligned} &O(1)te^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},\quad 0<t<1,\\
&O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},\quad t\geq 1.\end{aligned}\right.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(3.25)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial^{k}_{x}\Lambda_{2}^{*}(x,t)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial^{k}_{x}\left[\frac{e^{\beta^{*}_{2}t}}{\sqrt{4\pi\alpha^{*}_{2}t}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{4\alpha^{*}_{2}t}}\right]+O(1)te^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},&\quad 0<t<1,\\
&O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},&t\geq 1,\end{aligned}\right.\\
&\partial^{k+2}_{x}\Lambda_{2}^{*}(x,t)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial^{k+2}_{x}\left[\frac{e^{\beta^{*}_{2}t}}{\sqrt{4\pi\alpha^{*}_{2}t}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{4\alpha^{*}_{2}t}}\right]+tA_{2,1}\partial^{k}_{x}\left[\frac{e^{\beta^{*}_{2}t}}{\sqrt{4\pi\alpha^{*}_{2}t}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{4\alpha^{*}_{2}t}}\right]+O(1)te^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},&\quad 0<t<1,\\
&O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},&t\geq 1,\end{aligned}\right.\\
&\alpha^{*}_{2}=\frac{\mu}{v},\quad\beta^{*}_{2}=\frac{v\left(\mu pp_{e}+\kappa\theta_{e}p_{v}-\mu p_{v}\right)}{\mu\left(\mu-\kappa\theta_{e}\right)},\quad k=0,1.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.26)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial^{k}_{x}\Lambda_{3}^{*}(x,t)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial^{k}_{x}\left[\frac{e^{\beta^{*}_{3}t}}{\sqrt{4\pi\alpha^{*}_{3}t}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{4\alpha^{*}_{3}t}}\right]+O(1)te^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},&\quad 0<t<1,\\
&O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},&t\geq 1,\end{aligned}\right.\\
&\partial^{k+2}_{x}\Lambda_{3}^{*}(x,t)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial^{k+2}_{x}\left[\frac{e^{\beta^{*}_{3}t}}{\sqrt{4\pi\alpha^{*}_{3}t}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{4\alpha^{*}_{3}t}}\right]+tA_{3,1}\partial^{k}_{x}\left[\frac{e^{\beta^{*}_{3}t}}{\sqrt{4\pi\alpha^{*}_{3}t}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{4\alpha^{*}_{3}t}}\right]+O(1)te^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},&\quad 0<t<1,\\
&O(1)e^{-\sigma_{0}^{*}t-\sigma_{0}|x|},&t\geq 1,\end{aligned}\right.\\
&\alpha^{*}_{3}=\frac{\kappa\theta_{e}}{v},\quad\beta^{*}_{3}=\frac{pvp_{e}}{\kappa\theta_{e}-\mu},\quad k=0,1.\end{aligned}\right.

Proof.

The proof can be similarly constructed as in
[
15
]
. Here we use two steps to briefly sketch the proof.

\bullet
(Step 1:
\Lambda_{1}^{*}
) We first give the estimate of
\Lambda_{1}^{*}
. According to (
3.18
), we have

e^{\lambda_{1}^{*}t}=e^{\left(\frac{vp_{v}}{\mu}+\sum_{k=1}^{3}\frac{A_{1,k}}{(1+\eta^{2})^{k}}-\frac{K_{1}}{\left(\eta^{2}+1\right)^{4}}\right)t}=e^{\frac{vp_{v}}{\mu}t}e^{\left(\sum_{k=1}^{3}\frac{A_{1,k}}{(1+\eta^{2})^{k}}-\frac{K_{1}}{\left(\eta^{2}+1\right)^{4}}\right)t}.

Lemma
3.3
immediately implies that
e^{\lambda_{1}^{*}t}
is analytic in the region
|Im(\eta)|<\sigma_{0}
around the real axis. Moreover, we can view
e^{\lambda_{1}^{*}t}
as a function of
(1+\eta^{2})^{-1}
and expand
e^{\lambda_{1}^{*}t}
at
\eta=\infty
to obtain

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{1}^{*}t}=

\displaystyle e^{\frac{vp_{v}}{\mu}t}\left(1+\frac{A_{1,1}t}{(1+\eta^{2})}+\frac{\frac{A_{1,1}^{2}t^{2}}{2}-A_{1,1}t+A_{1,2}t}{(1+\eta^{2})^{2}}\right)
